# KITABU QUEST Grade 7 English

## Title Page

KITABU QUEST Grade 7 English

Kenya CBC content draft for review

Mascot: fox rabbit

This content-first draft was regenerated from Kitabu curriculum data using a topic-by-topic book plan. It is designed to help learners read, practise, discuss, create, and revise with confidence.

Cover artwork is intentionally deferred until all content has passed review and is marked ready-for-cover.

## How To Use This Book

This book helps Grade 7 learners study English through clear lessons, worked examples, activities, practice, and revision.

Use the inquiry questions to think first. Read the explanation. Try the guided example. Complete the activity. Answer the practice questions. Correct your work and ask for support where needed.

Read, speak, listen, write, and edit in connected language tasks.

Every unit has a local example, a misconception to avoid, success criteria, and a check for understanding.

## Learning Skills In This Book

Each chapter builds knowledge, skills, values, creativity, communication, collaboration, critical thinking, digital literacy, and self-confidence.

Look for these page types: Learn, Example, Activity, Practice, Reflection, Home Link, and Review.

How to study well: read the goal first, underline new words, try the example before reading the answer, discuss with a partner, and correct your work after feedback. Do not skip the reflection questions. They help you notice what you understand and what needs more practice.

## Conversational Skills: Polite Language: Lesson Opener

Learning area: Conversational Skills: Polite Language

Start here: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Conversational Skills: Polite Language.

Curriculum link: This lesson follows the Grade 7 curriculum sequence and focuses on Conversational Skills: Polite Language.

By the end of this lesson sequence, you should be able to:
- identify polite expressions used in the introduction of self and others
- use polite expressions in the introduction of self and others in different speaking contexts
- model respectful behaviour during introductions.

Inquiry questions:
- Why is it important for people to introduce themselves?

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Conversational Skills: Polite Language: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Conversational Skills: Polite Language.

Important idea: Conversational Skills: Polite Language.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Conversational Skills: Polite Language. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Polite language model for Conversational Skills: Polite Language: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to conversational skills: polite language, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

Key words:
- Conversational Skills: Polite Language
- Conversational
- Skills
- Polite
- Language

3. Prepare a one-minute spoken response with one point, one reason, and one example, then ask a partner for feedback on clarity and tone. Target outcome: model respectful behaviour during introductions.

## Reading 1.2.1 Extensive: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Reading 1.2.1 Extensive.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 1.2.1 Extensive. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 1.2.1 Extensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 1.2.1 Extensive
- Reading
- Extensive

## Word Classes: Nouns: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Word Classes: Nouns.

Important idea: Word Classes: Nouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: Nouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Nouns: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: nouns, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Word Classes: Nouns
- Classes
- Nouns

## Writing 1.5.1 Handwriting: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Writing 1.5.1 Handwriting.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Writing model for Writing 1.5.1 Handwriting: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 1.5.1 handwriting by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 1.5.1 handwriting by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

Key words:

- Writing
- Handwriting

In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 2.2.1 Intensive
- Reading
- Intensive

## Word Classes: Nouns: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Word Classes: Nouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Word Classes: Nouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Word Classes: Nouns: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to word classes: nouns, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Word Classes: Nouns
- Classes
- Nouns

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 2.5.1 mechanics by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 2.5.1 mechanics by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Listening for the main idea: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Listening for the main idea.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Listening for the main idea. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening for the main idea: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Key words:
- Listening for the main idea
- Listening

## Verbs and Tense: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Verbs and Tense.

Important idea: Verbs and Tense.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Verbs and Tense. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Verbs and Tense: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss verbs and tense yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed verbs and tense yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about verbs and tense: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Verbs and Tense

Listening model for Reading 3.4.1 Intensive: poem's structure in communicating a message.: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Writing improves when purpose, audience, structure, and editing work together. Plan the main idea first, arrange details in a sensible order, choose exact words, and revise at least one sentence after feedback.

Writing model for Writing 3.5.1 Writing: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 3.5.1 writing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 3.5.1 writing by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Your turn: Use the same plan for a paragraph, report, letter, or speech linked to this topic.

## Reading 4.2.1 Intensive: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Reading 4.2.1 Intensive.

Important idea: Reading 4.2.1 Intensive.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 4.2.1 Intensive. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 4.2.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 4.2.1 Intensive
- Reading
- Intensive

## Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: in the simple present and simple past: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: in the simple present and simple past.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: in the simple present and simple past. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: in the simple present and simple past: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss grammar 4.3.1 verbs: in the simple present and simple past yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed grammar 4.3.1 verbs: in the simple present and simple past yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about grammar 4.3.1 verbs: in the simple present and simple past: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: in the simple present and simple past

## Comparative and Superlative Adjectives: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Comparative and Superlative Adjectives.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Comparative and Superlative Adjectives. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Comparative and Superlative Adjectives: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to comparative and superlative adjectives, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Comparative and Superlative Adjectives
- Comparative
- Superlative
- Adjectives

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. 1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.

3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Writing model for Writing 5.5.1 Functional: Plan before writing.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 5.5.1 functional by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 5.5.1 functional by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Conversation Skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Conversation Skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions.

Important idea: Conversation Skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Conversation Skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Polite language model for Conversation Skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions: Match words to the setting and listener.

Situation: A learner is helping visitors during a school hospitality day. One visitor wants to reserve a table near a quiet corner.

Weak response: Sit there. I do not know if the table is free.

Improved response: Good afternoon. Let me check whether the quiet-corner table is available. If it has already been reserved, I can offer you another table near the window.

Why it works:
1. It opens politely.
2. It explains the action instead of sounding dismissive.
3. It gives an alternative.
4. It fits a hotel, dining, or public-service setting.

Your turn: Write a polite request, apology, or reservation response linked to conversation skills: verbal and non-verbal forms of polite interruptions, then underline the words that make the tone respectful.

Language model for Word Classes: Adverbs: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss word classes: adverbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed word classes: adverbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about word classes: adverbs: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

## Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details: Learn And Example

Begin with a real language task: a class discussion, reading club, letter, report, speech, or story situation linked to Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details.

Important idea: Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Key words:
- Listening Comprehension: Listening for Details
- Listening
- Comprehension
- Details

## Reading 7.2.1: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Reading 7.2.1.

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |

| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 7.2.1: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 7.2.1
- Reading

## Personal and possessive pronouns: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Personal and possessive pronouns.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Personal and possessive pronouns. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Personal and possessive pronouns: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to personal and possessive pronouns, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Personal and possessive pronouns
- Personal
- possessive
- pronouns

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Reading model for Reading 8.4.1 Intensive: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 8.5.1 composition by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Model paragraph: Our group studied writing 8.5.1 composition by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft. Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

## Reading 9.2.1: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Reading 9.2.1.

Reading well means tracking meaning, not just finishing words. Identify the main idea, notice how details support it, and answer with evidence from the text. For literature, connect character, setting, plot, language, and theme.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Reading 9.2.1. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Reading model for Reading 9.2.1: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Key words:
- Reading 9.2.1

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Reading model for Reading 11.2.1 Extensive: print or electronic non-fiction materials that are interesting: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Formation of Adjectives: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Formation of Adjectives.

Important idea: Formation of Adjectives.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Formation of Adjectives. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Formation of Adjectives: Sort words by class before using them in sentences.

Word set: Nairobi, chair, water, courage, several, quickly.

Model classification:
1. Nairobi is a proper noun because it names a specific place.
2. Chair is a common count noun because we can count one chair, two chairs.
3. Water is a common non-count noun in ordinary use.
4. Courage is an abstract noun because it names a quality.
5. Several is a determiner because it tells about quantity.
6. Quickly is an adverb because it describes how an action is done.

Model sentence: Several learners quickly carried the chair from Nairobi Hall after the reading activity.

Explanation: Word classes help us choose correct forms and place words where the sentence makes sense.

Your turn: Classify four words linked to formation of adjectives, then use two of them in correct sentences.

Key words:
- Formation of Adjectives

- Adjectives

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

Task: Write a short response that explains a learning activity connected to this topic.

Plan:
1. Purpose: explain what happened and what was learned.
2. Audience: classmates, teacher, parents, or public readers.
3. Opening sentence: Our group studied writing 11.5.1 spelling by reading the task, discussing evidence, and improving one draft.
4. Three details: what learners did, what evidence was used, and what changed after feedback.
5. Closing sentence: The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Each learner contributed one sentence, then the group checked word choice and punctuation. We revised the weakest sentence so the message became clearer. The activity showed that clear planning and revision improve communication.

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Listening: Views/ Opinions: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Listening: Views/ Opinions.

Speaking and listening are active skills. Listen for the main point, take brief notes, respond respectfully, and support your own point with a reason or example. Clear speech uses pace, volume, eye contact, and appropriate tone.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Listening model for Listening: Views/ Opinions: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

- Listening: Views/ Opinions

- use given phrasal verbs correctly in sentence construction
- acknowledge the role of phrasal verbs in communication.

Inquiry questions:
- Why should we use phrasal verbs correctly?

Before reading, write one thing you already know and one question you want this lesson to answer.

## Phrasal Verbs: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Phrasal Verbs.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Phrasal Verbs. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Phrasal Verbs: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss phrasal verbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed phrasal verbs yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about phrasal verbs: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Phrasal Verbs
- Phrasal
- Verbs

Listening model for Listening Comprehension: Extensive Listening: key issues raised in songs, addressing land travel: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

## Sentences: Simple Sentences: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Sentences: Simple Sentences.

Important idea: Sentences: Simple Sentences.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Sentences: Simple Sentences. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Sentences: Simple Sentences: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Your turn: Write one correct sentence about sentences: simple sentences, then underline the subject and verb.

Pronunciation and vocabulary model for Reading 13.4.1 Oral: purpose and occasions for which praise songs are: Hear, say, use, and check the word.

Word set: school, shade, share, shape.

1. Listen for the beginning sound.
2. Say each word clearly and slowly.
3. Use one word in a sentence: The learners sat under the shade during reading time.

Listening model for Listening 14.1.1 Consonant: listening 14.1.1 consonant in clear listening, speaking, reading: Catch key details from a recording or teacher-read text.

Transcript excerpt: "At dawn, Naliaka and her brother carried seedlings to the school garden. Their teacher reminded the club to water the young trees before the sun became hot. By Friday, every group had labelled its row and recorded the number of seedlings planted."

Question: Which details should a listener record?

Model answer:
1. Who: Naliaka, her brother, the teacher, and the club.
2. Where: the school garden.
3. What happened: seedlings were planted, watered, labelled, and recorded.
4. Message: careful listening helps us retell events in order.

Grade 7 check: A strong listening answer names people, place, event order, and message without inventing details.

Reading model for Reading 14.2.1 Study: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

## Subject- Verb Agreement: Learn And Example

Before answering, decide whether you are reading, speaking, listening, writing, or editing.

Important idea: Subject- Verb Agreement.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Subject- Verb Agreement. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Subject- Verb Agreement: Make the verb agree with the subject and time.

Original sentence: The reading group discuss subject-verb agreement yesterday and writes notes today.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject: the reading group.
2. Match the time words: yesterday needs past tense; today needs present tense.
3. Keep each verb in the correct form.
4. Improved sentence: The reading group discussed subject-verb agreement yesterday and writes notes today.

Explanation: Verb tense shows time, and agreement shows whether the subject is singular or plural.

Your turn: Write two correct sentences about subject-verb agreement: one in the past tense and one in the present tense.

Key words:
- Subject- Verb Agreement
- Agreement

Media writing model for Reading 14.4.1 Features: Turn facts into a short, lively article opening.

Task: Write the opening of a school news article about a clean-water campaign.

Weak opening: The campaign was good and many people came.

Improved opening: Learners at Baraka Secondary turned the assembly ground into a clean-water action centre on Friday, testing simple filters, interviewing health volunteers, and signing a pledge to report broken taps before water is wasted.

Why it works:
1. It names who, where, and when.
2. It uses active verbs: turned, testing, interviewing, signing.
3. It gives concrete details instead of saying only good.
4. It fits a public audience.

Reading model for Reading 15.2.1 Reading: Read the short passage, then answer with evidence.

Passage: Amina borrowed a library book about a school clean-water campaign. She first skimmed the headings, then read the middle paragraph slowly because it explained why learners labelled taps, reported leaks, and kept drinking cups clean. In her notebook, she wrote one main idea and two details from the paragraph instead of guessing from the title alone.

Question: What does Amina's behaviour show about careful reading?

Model answer:
1. Point: Amina reads carefully and uses evidence.
2. Evidence: She skims headings, rereads the paragraph, and records one main idea with two details.
3. Explanation: These actions show that a strong reading answer comes from the text, not from a quick guess.

Grade 7 check: A strong answer names the idea, quotes or paraphrases evidence, and explains why the evidence matters.

- Reading 15.2.1 Reading

## Affirmative and Negative Sentences: Learn And Example

Think of the listener or reader first. The task is connected to Affirmative and Negative Sentences.

Important idea: Affirmative and Negative Sentences.

Grammar helps meaning become clear. Do not memorise a rule alone; test it inside a real sentence. Check who or what the sentence is about, what action or state is shown, and how punctuation guides the reader.

A common mistake is memorising rules without using them in meaningful speech, reading, or writing. Avoid it by reading the model first, underlining useful words, and improving one sentence after feedback.

Success criteria:
- meaning is clear
- language choice fits the task
- spelling, punctuation, and grammar are checked

For Grade 7, use clear sentences, examples, and respectful discussion.

Inline visual support:

| Communication part | What to show |
| --- | --- |
| Purpose | The reading, listening, speaking, writing, or grammar task in Affirmative and Negative Sentences. |
| Audience | Who needs to understand the message. |
| Evidence or example | A detail from the passage, conversation, notice, report, or spoken task. |
| Revision | One sentence improved for clarity, tone, spelling, punctuation, or grammar. |

Language model for Affirmative and Negative Sentences: Improve a sentence so the meaning is precise.

Draft sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation need one reason.

Revision steps:
1. Find the subject of the second clause: the explanation.
2. Check the verb form: explanation is singular, so use needs.
3. Keep the correction small so the meaning stays the same.
4. Improved sentence: The learner wrote clear examples, but the explanation needs one reason.

Explanation: Correct agreement and exact word order help the reader understand the idea without guessing.

Your turn: Write one correct sentence about affirmative and negative sentences, then underline the subject and verb.

Key words:
- Affirmative and Negative Sentences
- Affirmative
- Negative
- Sentences

## Glossary

Use this glossary to revise important words in English.

- Conversational Skills: Polite Language: A language topic that should be practised through reading, speaking, listening, writing, and editing in a real task.
- Reading 1.2.1 Extensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Word Classes: Nouns: A language-control skill: use correct word forms, sentence structure, and punctuation so meaning is clear.
- Reading 1.4.1: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Writing 1.5.1 Handwriting: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.
- Oral Presentations: Oral Narratives: A communication skill: listen for the main idea and details, then respond clearly with a reason or example.
- Reading 2.2.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Reading 2.4.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Writing 2.5.1 Mechanics: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.
- Listening for the main idea: A communication skill: listen for the main idea and details, then respond clearly with a reason or example.
- Reading 3.2.1. Reading: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Verbs and Tense: A language-control skill: use correct word forms, sentence structure, and punctuation so meaning is clear.
- Reading 3.4.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Writing 3.5.1 Writing: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.
- Listening Comprehension: Selective Listening: A communication skill: listen for the main idea and details, then respond clearly with a reason or example.
- Reading 4.2.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Grammar 4.3.1 Verbs: A language-control skill: use correct word forms, sentence structure, and punctuation so meaning is clear.
- Reading 4.4.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Writing 4.5.1 Paragraphing: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.
- Pronunciation: Sounds and Word Stress: A speaking and word-use skill: say the word clearly, understand its meaning, and use it correctly in a sentence.
- Reading 5.2.1 Synonyms: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Comparative and Superlative Adjectives: A language-control skill: use correct word forms, sentence structure, and punctuation so meaning is clear.
- Reading 5.4.1 Intensive: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Writing 5.5.1 Functional: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.
- Conversation Skills: A language topic that should be practised through reading, speaking, listening, writing, and editing in a real task.
- Reading 6.2.1 Reading: A reading skill: identify meaning, use evidence from the text, and explain how details support the answer.
- Word Classes: Adverbs: A language-control skill: use correct word forms, sentence structure, and punctuation so meaning is clear.
- Reading 6.4.1 Oral: A communication skill: listen for the main idea and details, then respond clearly with a reason or example.
- Writing 6.5.1 Commonly: A writing skill: plan for purpose and audience, organise ideas, and edit spelling, grammar, punctuation, and meaning.

Add five more words from your lessons, then write a meaning and one correct example sentence or worked example for each word.

## Answer And Teacher Notes

A strong English answer should:

- match the purpose and audience;
- use evidence from the passage, discussion, task, or real context;
- organise ideas into clear sentences and paragraphs;
- choose words that fit the tone;
- edit spelling, punctuation, grammar, and meaning after feedback.

For reading questions, use point, evidence, and explanation. For writing tasks, plan before writing and revise at least one sentence before submitting.

Answer-key guide: reading answers need a point, a quoted or paraphrased detail, and an explanation. Grammar answers need the corrected sentence and the reason for the correction. Writing answers need purpose, audience, structure, and editing evidence. Oral tasks need a clear position, reason, example, and respectful close.

Rubric for self-checking:
- 4: The answer addresses the exact question, uses subject vocabulary correctly, gives evidence or working, and includes a useful check or correction.
- 3: The main answer is correct but one part needs stronger evidence, clearer working, better labels, or a fuller explanation.
- 2: Some understanding is shown, but the answer is incomplete, too general, or hard to follow.
- 1: The answer is guessed, off topic, or missing the evidence needed for the task.

Correction routine: reread the command word, underline the key data or evidence, improve one weak step, and write one sentence explaining why the final answer is reasonable.

## Final Project

Create a final project for English.

Your project must include:
1. A clear title.
2. A real-life problem or question.
3. Evidence from at least three lessons.
4. A drawing, table, map, chart, model, dialogue, performance plan, artwork note, or worked solution.
5. A short reflection explaining what you learned.

Present your project to a classmate, teacher, parent, or study group.
